I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Yes, thank you for seeing me. Well, it’s a bit of a long story, really. I’ve been working in retail for years—I’m a supervisor at a fairly large store, and you know how incredibly stressful that can be, especially lately with all the staffing shortages and having to constantly cover shifts. I try to hold it all together, I really do, because I’ve always prided myself on being responsible and capable. I mean, I have my degree, I know how to manage people, but recently... I don't know, things have just felt so incredibly heavy. \n\nI’ve been pushing through, going to work, doing the schedules, dealing with endless customer complaints, but it’s like wading through molasses just to get through a normal Tuesday. But what finally scared me into actually picking up the phone and making this appointment—which took me a good week to do, honestly, because I kept putting it off thinking I’d just snap out of it—is that I’ve been having these... thoughts. \n\nIt’s not like me at all. But I’ll be lying in bed, dreading the alarm going off, and I catch myself thinking things like, \"If I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, that might be easier.\" Just to have some peace and quiet, you know? It really, really frightened me to realize my brain was going there, even though I absolutely do not want to hurt myself. I would never do anything like that. But just the fact that the thought of simply not waking up sounded like a relief... that’s when I knew I needed to come in and talk to someone. Because I just can't keep going on like this, pretending everything is fine when I'm having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things that used to bring you pleasure, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually, and it’s been incredibly frustrating for me because I’ve always been such a creative person. Even with the store taking up so much of my time and energy, I always made a point to keep up with my art. I have this lovely little sunroom at the back of my house where the light is just perfect in the late afternoons, and I usually love going in there. \n\nSo lately, I’ll try to force myself to keep up with it. I’ll go through the whole routine—I’ll get my water cup, I’ll set out all my paints... my watercolors, usually, because they’re easier to clean up after a long shift. Or I’ll think about heading down to the community center for this pottery class I’ve been taking for years with a few of the ladies from my neighborhood. But... I don't know, it's like, when I actually sit down, or when I get to the studio, I just stare at the materials. \n\nI just look at the clay or the brushes, and I sit there waiting for that feeling, you know? That little rush of inspiration or joy that usually hits me when I'm about to make something. But there's just... nothing. I feel absolutely none of the spark I used to feel. It’s almost like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby. And I’ll sit there for maybe twenty, thirty minutes, just staring at the blank paper, hoping it’ll come back, but everything just feels so incredibly flat. I used to lose whole weekends in that sunroom, just painting whatever came to mind, and now it just feels like another chore on my endless to-do list, which really breaks my heart because my art used to be my one real escape from all the madness at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time in the past where you felt this way for more than just a few days—like, more than two weeks—before today’s appointment?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, yes. Much, much longer than two weeks. I would say this... this low, flat part, this heaviness I was describing, it’s been present for about six months now. I remember quite clearly because it started right around the time we were doing the big seasonal floor set at the store, which is always a massive undertaking, but usually, I bounce back after I have a weekend to recover. But this time, I just didn't. \n\nIt’s been half a year of just feeling completely depleted. And it’s been going on long enough that my usual routines—the things I’ve relied on for years and years to keep me grounded—have completely stopped feeling restorative. Like the art classes I was just telling you about. I still try to go through the motions, but it does absolutely nothing for me. And it’s the exact same story with my morning walks. \n\nI’ve been an early riser my whole adult life, and I used to absolutely cherish that quiet time before the neighborhood wakes up. I’d put on my good walking shoes—the ones with the extra arch support, because standing on those hard commercial tile floors all day really does a number on your knees and lower back when you're in your sixties—and I’d do my forty-five-minute loop around the botanical park near my subdivision. I used to come back to the house feeling so refreshed, you know? Just ready to face the day and whatever scheduling disasters were waiting for me at work. \n\nBut for the past six months, I’ll do the exact same walk, take the exact same path past the duck pond, and I just feel... nothing. I'm just trudging along, putting one foot in front of the other, waiting to feel that little lift in my spirit, but it never comes. The fresh air doesn't help, the sunshine doesn't help. It’s just this persistent, flat feeling that absolutely will not lift, no matter how strictly I stick to my routines. I kept telling myself, \"Just give it another week, just keep walking, keep setting up your paints, it'll pass,\" but here we are six whole months later, and it’s just as heavy as it was back in the spring. It's exhausting, honestly, trying to maintain all these habits when the payoff is just completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, have you ever had another time in your life where you felt this persistently low and lost interest in things for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... You know, it's funny you ask, because when you're in the middle of feeling this way, it almost feels like you've always felt this way, doesn't it? It just sort of colors all your memories. But if I really sit down and think back over the years—and I've had a long career in retail, like I said, going on thirty-five years now, starting from a floor associate all the way up to supervisor—I honestly don't think I've ever had a stretch quite like this. \n\nI mean, I've had bad days, of course. Who hasn't? Especially around the holidays. Black Friday alone is enough to make anyone want to pull the covers over their head and never come out. I remember back in, oh, it must have been 2015, we had this awful regional manager who made everyone's life miserable for about a month before he was finally transferred, and I was definitely stressed and tired then. I'd come home and just crash on the sofa. But I always bounced back. Give me a Sunday off with a good cup of coffee and my watercolors, and I was right as rain by Monday morning. \n\nSo, no, I don't think I've ever had a time where I felt this completely empty and disconnected from my life for weeks on end. Not before this current stretch. This feels entirely new, and that's part of what's so unnerving about it, honestly. I've always been the reliable one, the one who powers through, the one everyone at the store looks to when things go wrong, and to just have no energy or interest in anything... it's just not me at all.\n\nDoctor: Since this current six-month period is the first time you’ve felt this persistently low and disconnected, have you had any other times in your life — even if shorter or less severe — where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for more than two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: No, I... honestly, no. I really haven't. And I’ve been racking my brain since we started talking, trying to be completely thorough with you, because I know how important it is to get the full picture with these things. But even if I look back at the really, truly stressful chapters of my life... I mean, like when I first became a supervisor. I was in my late thirties, I think, and the learning curve was just absolutely tremendous. I was suddenly managing people who used to be my peers, which is always such a delicate dance, you know? And I was working these awful, unpredictable split shifts because our store manager at the time just couldn't get the scheduling right to save his life. \n\nI was exhausted then, certainly. I felt worn down to the bone some days. But it wasn't... it wasn't *this*. Even then, I never lost my joy in things. I never felt hopeless. Even during the absolute worst of the holiday rushes over the last three decades, when I'm working six days a week and dealing with just the most irate customers trying to return damaged merchandise without receipts, I always still looked forward to my downtime. I'd come home, take my shoes off, and I'd still want to read a good book or work on a watercolor. I still wanted to talk to my friends and go to my pottery classes. \n\nMy mother used to say I was like a rubber band—I just always snapped right back into shape. If I had a bad week, it was just that: a bad week. By day three or four, I'd have a good cry or vent to my sister on the phone for an hour, and I'd be completely fine. So, no, I've never had a time, not for two weeks or more, where I just didn't care about anything or felt this down. Not even a milder version of it. It’s really just been this one, long, miserable six-month stretch we're in right now, which is why I finally decided I needed to come see someone about it. I just don't know how to snap back this time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other periods in your life — even if shorter or less intense than the one you’ve been describing — where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for more than two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I know we literally just went over this, doctor, but no, really, I haven't. I promise I'm not just brushing the question off or trying to rush through it—I know how thorough you need to be to figure this out. But like I was just saying, it really has only ever been this current six-month stretch. \n\nI’ve always been such a resilient person, you see. I mean, I could give you a hundred examples of incredibly stressful times where anyone else might have just given up. Like back in 2018, when corporate decided to completely overhaul our entire point-of-sale software right in the middle of the back-to-school rush. Oh, it was an absolute nightmare. I had teenage cashiers practically in tears because the new registers kept freezing up with lines stretching all the way back to the shoe department, and I was working twelve, sometimes thirteen-hour days just trying to keep the front end from completely falling apart. I was dog-tired, absolutely. My feet throbbed every single night, and I practically lived on ibuprofen and black coffee. \n\nBut even then, with all that immense pressure, I never felt *hopeless*. I didn't lose my spark. I'd still go home and look forward to my weekend, or I'd still want to get my hands in some clay at my pottery class, or I'd be excited to try a new recipe for dinner. I've always been so good at leaving my stress at the store and just enjoying my life once I clocked out. So, no, to answer your question again, I've never had a period of two weeks or more where I felt down or lost interest in things like this. Not ever. This whole heavy, empty experience since the spring is completely uncharted territory for me, which is why it's been so frightening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current six-month period when you experienced a major depressive episode — meaning you felt down, lost interest in things, or had other symptoms most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like I'm taking a test where I keep getting the exact same question, just dressed up a little differently each time! I really, truly mean it when I say no. I haven't. I know you have to ask these things to be absolutely certain, but I promise you, I'm not hiding anything from you or forgetting anything. I've never had a time like that before this current stretch. Not for two weeks, not most of the day... never. \n\nI mean, I don't know what else I can tell you to paint the picture. I've lived a very full sixty-two years, and sure, it hasn't all been a walk in the park. I remember way back when I was getting my bachelor's degree, and I was already working retail full-time to pay for it. I’d be up until two in the morning writing papers, and then I’d have to be on the floor opening the store by seven. I was running on absolute fumes, just completely depleted. I remember once I literally cried in the breakroom because the vending machine ate my dollar, just because my nerves were so frayed from being tired! But even then, in the thick of all that stress, it wasn't this... this heavy, dark thing you're asking about. I still loved my classes. I still enjoyed chatting with my regular customers. I never lost my interest in living my life. \n\nSo, no, I've never had a period where I felt down nearly every day for two weeks. This whole thing, these last six months... it's the very first time my mind has ever done this to me, which is why I'm sitting here talking to you today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this six-month stretch when you experienced a major depressive episode — meaning you felt down, lost interest in things, or had other symptoms most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... am I misunderstanding the question somehow? Or is it just standard medical procedure to ask this four times in a row? Because I really, truly do not have a different answer to give you. No. Never. Before this spring, I have never, ever had a two-week period of feeling down or losing interest in my life. \n\nI mean, I could sit here and tell you my whole life story, chapter and verse, and you wouldn't find a single time. Take, for instance, back in... oh, it must have been 2011, when our new district manager decided we needed to do a full physical store inventory the very week after Thanksgiving. The week after Thanksgiving! Anyone in retail will tell you that is absolute madness. We had pallets of seasonal merchandise in the back room that hadn't even been processed yet, and I was at the store until three in the morning scanning barcodes with this ancient, heavy scanner that kept dying on me every twenty minutes. I didn't see my husband for what felt like a month—bless his heart, he just kept leaving plates of dinner wrapped in tin foil in the fridge for me so I'd remember to eat. \n\nI was stressed to the absolute limit, yes. But I wasn't... I wasn't depressed. I didn't lose my joy. I'd still listen to my mystery novels on tape on the dark drive home and actually enjoy them, trying to figure out who committed the crime before the detective did. I still felt exactly like *me*. So, I really don't know how else to say it to you, but no. I have never had a period like that before. Just this current six-month stretch. That is really, honestly it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this six-month stretch where you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I've answered this so many times now. I feel like you're waiting for me to suddenly remember some terrible, dark period of my life that I've somehow completely blocked out, but I'm telling you, it just doesn't exist! \n\nI've been racking my brain while you've been asking, trying to think if maybe there was some tiny blip I forgot. Like, what about when my mother passed away back in '98? That was awful, of course. I was so sad. I cried for days. I took a week off work, which I never do, and I just sat in her house sorting through all her old sewing patterns and crying. But even then, it was grief, you know? It was normal. It wasn't this... this heavy, suffocating blanket that's been on me since the spring. After a couple of weeks, I went back to work. I started smiling again when my favorite customers came in. I started baking again. The sadness was there, but it didn't steal my whole life away. \n\nSo, I'm begging you, please believe me. I have never, in my sixty-two years on this earth, had a period of two weeks or more where I felt down or lost interest in things like this. Never. This is the first time. I don't know how else to convince you, but that is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current six-month period when you felt down, lost interest in things, or experienced other symptoms of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I have answered this. I have answered this exact same question, what, four times now? Five? I'm losing count. \n\nI don't know what you want me to say! I had to rely on my husband to ferry me back and forth to the store, and he's not exactly the most punctual man on the planet, bless him. I was irritated. I was annoyed. But I wasn't *depressed*. I didn't lose my spark. I sat in the breakroom with my foot elevated on a stack of printer paper, barking orders and doing the scheduling, and I still felt like myself. \n\nI just... I don't understand why we keep circling back to this. I'm a sixty-two-year-old woman who has worked retail her entire adult life. I know stress. I know exhaustion. I know sadness. But I did not know *this* until six months ago. So please, please, can we just accept that the answer is no and move forward? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I actually cannot believe this. Are you doing this on purpose? Because if this is some sort of psychological test to see if I'll finally crack and start screaming, you are getting awfully close, Doctor. 

I feel like I'm dealing with our old point-of-sale registers back in 2014. We had this terrible software update—corporate pushed it through right before the back-to-school rush, which is exactly the kind of brilliant timing you'd expect from people sitting in office chairs who never actually have to work the floor—and the screens would just glitch. You'd scan a pack of highlighters, and the screen would prompt, 'Apply rewards number?' You'd hit no. Then it would ask, 'Apply rewards number?' again. Over and over and over. I had to stand there with a line of twenty angry mothers holding screaming toddlers, hitting the 'No' button on that screen until my finger practically went numb, just praying the cash drawer would finally pop open so we could move on with our lives! 

That is exactly what this feels like right now. I am hitting the 'No' button for you, Doctor. No! No, I have never had a two-week period of feeling down or losing interest before this spring. No, I did not have a major depressive whatever-you-called-it. I didn't have it in my twenties, I didn't have it in my thirties, I didn't have it when my kids left for college, and I don't have a different answer for you now than I did five minutes ago. 

Look, I am a very patient woman. You do not survive decades in retail management without the patience of a literal saint. I have smiled through things that would make most people weep. But I am telling you, for the absolute final time, there is nothing else to find in my past. The heavy, awful feeling started six months ago, and before that, my brain was perfectly fine. That's it. Can we please, please, for the love of heaven, talk about something else now? Because if you ask me that exact same question one more time, I think I might actually just get up and walk right out that door.

If I had a bad week, it was just that: a bad week. By day three or four, I'd have a good cry or vent to my sister on the phone for an hour, and I'd be completely fine. So, no, I've never had a time, not for two weeks or more, where I just didn't care about anything or felt this down. Not even a milder version of it. It’s really just been this one, long, miserable six-month stretch we're in right now, which is why I finally decided I needed to come see someone about it. I just don't know how to snap back this time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other periods in your life — even if shorter or less intense than the one you’ve been describing — where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for more than two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I know we literally just went over this, doctor, but no, really, I haven't. I promise I'm not just brushing the question off or trying to rush through it—I know how thorough you need to be to figure this out. But like I was just saying, it really has only ever been this current six-month stretch. \n\nI’ve always been such a resilient person, you see. I mean, I could give you a hundred examples of incredibly stressful times where anyone else might have just given up. Like back in 2018, when corporate decided to completely overhaul our entire point-of-sale software right in the middle of the back-to-school rush. Oh, it was an absolute nightmare. I had teenage cashiers practically in tears because the new registers kept freezing up with lines stretching all the way back to the shoe department, and I was working twelve, sometimes thirteen-hour days just trying to keep the front end from completely falling apart. I was dog-tired, absolutely. My feet throbbed every single night, and I practically lived on ibuprofen and black coffee. \n\nBut even then, with all that immense pressure, I never felt *hopeless*. I didn't lose my spark. I'd still go home and look forward to my weekend, or I'd still want to get my hands in some clay at my pottery class, or I'd be excited to try a new recipe for dinner. I've always been so good at leaving my stress at the store and just enjoying my life once I clocked out. So, no, to answer your question again, I've never had a period of two weeks or more where I felt down or lost interest in things like this. Not ever. This whole heavy, empty experience since the spring is completely uncharted territory for me, which is why it's been so frightening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current six-month period when you experienced a major depressive episode — meaning you felt down, lost interest in things, or had other symptoms most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like I'm taking a test where I keep getting the exact same question, just dressed up a little differently each time! I really, truly mean it when I say no. I haven't. I know you have to ask these things to be absolutely certain, but I promise you, I'm not hiding anything from you or forgetting anything. I've never had a time like that before this current stretch. Not for two weeks, not most of the day... never. \n\nI mean, I don't know what else I can tell you to paint the picture. I've lived a very full sixty-two years, and sure, it hasn't all been a walk in the park. I remember way back when I was getting my bachelor's degree, and I was already working retail full-time to pay for it. I’d be up until two in the morning writing papers, and then I’d have to be on the floor opening the store by seven. I was running on absolute fumes, just completely depleted. I remember once I literally cried in the breakroom because the vending machine ate my dollar, just because my nerves were so frayed from being tired! But even then, in the thick of all that stress, it wasn't this... this heavy, dark thing you're asking about. I still loved my classes. I still enjoyed chatting with my regular customers. I never lost my interest in living my life. \n\nSo, no, I've never had a period where I felt down nearly every day for two weeks. This whole thing, these last six months... it's the very first time my mind has ever done this to me, which is why I'm sitting here talking to you today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this six-month stretch when you experienced a major depressive episode — meaning you felt down, lost interest in things, or had other symptoms most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... am I misunderstanding the question somehow? Or is it just standard medical procedure to ask this four times in a row? Because I really, truly do not have a different answer to give you. No. Never. Before this spring, I have never, ever had a two-week period of feeling down or losing interest in my life. \n\nI mean, I could sit here and tell you my whole life story, chapter and verse, and you wouldn't find a single time. Take, for instance, back in... oh, it must have been 2011, when our new district manager decided we needed to do a full physical store inventory the very week after Thanksgiving. The week after Thanksgiving! Anyone in retail will tell you that is absolute madness. We had pallets of seasonal merchandise in the back room that hadn't even been processed yet, and I was at the store until three in the morning scanning barcodes with this ancient, heavy scanner that kept dying on me every twenty minutes. I didn't see my husband for what felt like a month—bless his heart, he just kept leaving plates of dinner wrapped in tin foil in the fridge for me so I'd remember to eat. \n\nI was stressed to the absolute limit, yes. But I wasn't... I wasn't depressed. I didn't lose my joy. I'd still listen to my mystery novels on tape on the dark drive home and actually enjoy them, trying to figure out who committed the crime before the detective did. I still felt exactly like *me*. So, I really don't know how else to say it to you, but no. I have never had a period like that before. Just this current six-month stretch. That is really, honestly it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this six-month stretch where you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I just... I don't know what to say anymore. I really don't. Are you... is there something in my chart that says I have? Because I feel like I'm going crazy here. I've answered this. I've answered this so many times now. I feel like you're waiting for me to suddenly remember some terrible, dark period of my life that I've somehow completely blocked out, but I'm telling you, it just doesn't exist! \n\nI've been racking my brain while you've been asking, trying to think if maybe there was some tiny blip I forgot. Like, what about when my mother passed away back in '98? That was awful, of course. I was so sad. I cried for days. I took a week off work, which I never do, and I just sat in her house sorting through all her old sewing patterns and crying. But even then, it was grief, you know? It was normal. It wasn't this... this heavy, suffocating blanket that's been on me since the spring. After a couple of weeks, I went back to work. I started smiling again when my favorite customers came in. I started baking again. The sadness was there, but it didn't steal my whole life away. \n\nSo, I'm begging you, please believe me. I have never, in my sixty-two years on this earth, had a period of two weeks or more where I felt down or lost interest in things like this. Never. This is the first time. I don't know how else to convince you, but that is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current six-month period when you felt down, lost interest in things, or experienced other symptoms of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I am honestly at my wit's end here. I really am. I feel like I'm on some kind of hidden camera show, or maybe I'm speaking a completely different language and I just don't realize it. Do you want me to just make something up so we can move on? Because I will! You'd scan a pack of highlighters, and the screen would prompt, 'Apply rewards number?' You'd hit no. Then it would ask, 'Apply rewards number?' again. Over and over and over. I had to stand there with a line of twenty angry mothers holding screaming toddlers, hitting the 'No' button on that screen until my finger practically went numb, just praying the cash drawer would finally pop open so we could move on with our lives! \n\nThat is exactly what this feels like right now. Because I just can't keep going on like this, pretending everything is fine when I'm having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things that used to bring you pleasure, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually, and it’s been incredibly frustrating for me because I’ve always been such a creative person. Even with the store taking up so much of my time and energy, I always made a point to keep up with my art. I have this lovely little sunroom at the back of my house where the light is just perfect in the late afternoons, and I usually love going in there. \n\nSo lately, I’ll try to force myself to keep up with it. I’ll go through the whole routine—I’ll get my water cup, I’ll set out all my paints... my watercolors, usually, because they’re easier to clean up after a long shift. Or I’ll think about heading down to the community center for this pottery class I’ve been taking for years with a few of the ladies from my neighborhood. But... I don't know, it's like, when I actually sit down, or when I get to the studio, I just stare at the materials. \n\nI just look at the clay or the brushes, and I sit there waiting for that feeling, you know? That little rush of inspiration or joy that usually hits me when I'm about to make something. But there's just... nothing. I feel absolutely none of the spark I used to feel. It’s almost like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby. And I’ll sit there for maybe twenty, thirty minutes, just staring at the blank paper, hoping it’ll come back, but everything just feels so incredibly flat. I used to lose whole weekends in that sunroom, just painting whatever came to mind, and now it just feels like another chore on my endless to-do list, which really breaks my heart because my art used to be my one real escape from all the madness at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time in the past where you felt this way for more than just a few days—like, more than two weeks—before today’s appointment?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, yes. But this time, I just didn't. \n\nIt’s been half a year of just feeling completely depleted. And it’s been going on long enough that my usual routines—the things I’ve relied on for years and years to keep me grounded—have completely stopped feeling restorative. Like the art classes I was just telling you about. I still try to go through the motions, but it does absolutely nothing for me. And it’s the exact same story with my morning walks. \n\nI’ve been an early riser my whole adult life, and I used to absolutely cherish that quiet time before the neighborhood wakes up. Just ready to face the day and whatever scheduling disasters were waiting for me at work. \n\nBut for the past six months, I’ll do the exact same walk, take the exact same path past the duck pond, and I just feel... nothing. I had to stand there with a line of twenty angry mothers holding screaming toddlers, hitting the 'No' button on that screen until my finger practically went numb, just praying the cash drawer would finally pop open so we could move on with our lives! \n\nThat is exactly what this feels like right now. I am hitting the 'No' button for you, Doctor. No! No, I have never had a two-week period of feeling down or losing interest before this spring. No, I did not have a major depressive whatever-you-called-it. I didn't have it in my twenties, I didn't have it in my thirties, I didn't have it when my kids left for college, and I don't have a different answer for you now than I did five minutes ago. \n\nLook, I am a very patient woman. You do not survive decades in retail management without the patience of a literal saint. I have smiled through things that would make most people weep. But I am telling you, for the absolute final time, there is nothing else to find in my past. The heavy, awful feeling started six months ago, and before that, my brain was perfectly fine. That's it. Can we please, please, for the love of heaven, talk about something else now? Because if you ask me that exact same question one more time, I think I might actually just get up and walk right out that door.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time before this current six-month period when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I... I am actually speechless. Well, no, I'm not speechless, obviously, because I have a very clear opinion on what is happening right now, but I am just... I am absolutely flabbergasted. \n\nAre you a robot? Is this some sort of artificial intelligence experiment they're running at this clinic? Because I told you exactly what I would do if you asked me that one more time, and you just looked me dead in the eye and read the exact same sentence off your little notepad! Where is my purse? I am actually grabbing my purse right now. \n\nI train my shift supervisors to actively listen. Active listening! It's module four in our corporate training binder. You look the person in the eye, you process what they are saying, and you respond to the information they just gave you. If I had a trainee on the floor who asked a customer the same question five times in a row after the customer explicitly answered it and threatened to leave, I would have them off the register and in the back office for a write-up before they could blink! You do not just read off a script like a broken toy when a human being is sitting right in front of you, pouring their heart out!\n\nFor the absolute, unequivocal, final time before I walk out of this office and demand my copay back from your receptionist: NO. N-O. No! I did not have a two-week period. I did not have a three-week period. I did not feel down or lose interest in my life before this spring. My life was perfectly fine, my mood was perfectly fine, I was perfectly fine until six months ago! \n\nI came here because I am struggling *now*. I need help with what is happening to me *now*, with this awful, suffocating weight that has been sitting on my chest since the spring, and instead, I am sitting here being interrogated about a past that literally does not exist. Do you have a hearing problem? Is there a glitch on your computer screen? Because I honestly cannot fathom what is happening in this room right now. I don't have a different answer for you! The answer is no!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this current six-month stretch when you felt down, lost interest in things, or experienced other symptoms of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: That is it. I am standing up. I am literally standing up right now with my purse on my shoulder. You are... I mean, you are beyond belief. \n\nI have been in retail and customer service for forty years. Forty years! Since the days when we had to hand-write receipts and use those clunky carbon-copy credit card sliders, you know, the ones that made that awful 'ka-chunk, ka-chunk' sound? I have dealt with people trying to return half-eaten boxes of cereal. I had a woman back in 1998 scream at me for twenty solid minutes because her coupon was expired by five years and she insisted we honor it anyway. I have seen every level of stubborn, unlistening, impossible human behavior there is to see on this earth. But you? You take the cake. You absolutely take the cake. \n\nI don't know if you're writing down 'patient is uncooperative' or 'patient is hysterical' on that little pad of yours, but if you are, you make sure you also note that the doctor asked the exact same question half a dozen times in a row like a skipping record! \n\nFor the absolute final time, as I am walking toward your door: No. I did not have a period where I felt down before six months ago. I didn't lose interest in things. I didn't have these symptoms for two weeks. I didn't have them for two days! I was completely, one hundred percent fine before this spring. I came here for help with the heavy fog I am in *right now*, which I have already told you about. But clearly, you are stuck in some kind of bizarre time loop and aren't actually going to listen to a word I say. \n\nThe answer is no. Just... no! I'm going to the front desk. I really am."
